<a id='question-060'></a>

### Question 060: Folate Metabolism & One-Carbon Transfer
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of tetrahydrofolate (THF) derivatives in one-carbon metabolism, specifically the transfer of formyl groups, and the consequences of inhibiting this process. (理解四氢叶酸 (THF) 衍生物在单碳代谢中的作用，特别是甲酰基的转移，以及抑制该过程的后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of rheumatoid arthritis presents to the emergency department complaining of severe fatigue, shortness of breath, and dizziness. He has been taking methotrexate (MTX) 20 mg weekly for the past 6 months to manage his arthritis. His wife reports that he has recently developed glossitis and mouth sores. Physical examination reveals pallor, tachycardia (110 bpm), and a slightly enlarged spleen. Laboratory results show:
- Hemoglobin: 8.5 g/dL (Normal: 13.5-17.5 g/dL)
- MCV: 110 fL (Normal: 80-100 fL)
- Platelet count: 150,000/µL (Normal: 150,000-450,000/µL)
- White blood cell count: 3,000/µL (Normal: 4,500-11,000/µL)
- Serum Folate: < 5 ng/mL (Normal: > 4 ng/mL)
- Serum Methotrexate level: 2.5 µg/L (Therapeutic range: 0.5-1.0 µg/L)
- Red blood cell dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR) activity: Significantly inhibited.
- Bone marrow biopsy: Hypercellular with megaloblastic changes.

Which of the following biochemical processes is most directly inhibited by methotrexate, leading to the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) The conversion of serine to glycine.
(B) The conversion of deoxyuridine monophosphate (dUMP) to deoxythymidine monophosphate (dTMP).
(C) The conversion of homocysteine to methionine.
(D) The conversion of formyl-THF to methylene-THF.
(E) The conversion of glutamate to glutamine.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with rheumatoid arthritis.
- **Medication:** Methotrexate (MTX), an immunosuppressant and chemotherapy agent.
- **Symptoms:** Fatigue, shortness of breath, dizziness, glossitis, mouth sores (suggestive of anemia and mucositis).
- **Physical Exam:** Pallor, tachycardia, splenomegaly (consistent with anemia).
- **Lab Findings:**
    - Low Hemoglobin (8.5 g/dL) and high MCV (110 fL): Macrocytic anemia.
    - Low Platelets (150,000/µL) and low WBC (3,000/µL): Pancytopenia.
    - Low Serum Folate (< 5 ng/mL): Folate deficiency.
    - High Serum Methotrexate level (2.5 µg/L): Methotrexate toxicity.
    - Inhibited Red blood cell DHFR activity: Confirms MTX mechanism.
    - Hypercellular bone marrow with megaloblastic changes: Characteristic of impaired DNA synthesis due to folate deficiency.
- **Deduction:** The patient presents with signs and symptoms of severe folate deficiency (macrocytic anemia, pancytopenia, glossitis, mucositis) likely caused by methotrexate toxicity. Methotrexate is known to inhibit dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR). The question asks for the biochemical process most directly inhibited by MTX, leading to this presentation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Methotrexate (MTX) is a structural analog of folic acid. Its primary mechanism of action is the potent and specific inhibition of dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR).
- DHFR is a crucial enzyme in the folate cycle. It catalyzes the reduction of dihydrofolate (DHF) to tetrahydrofolate (THF) using NADPH as a cofactor.
- THF is the active form of folate and serves as a carrier of one-carbon units (e.g., methyl, methylene, formyl, formimino groups) essential for various biosynthetic reactions, including purine and thymidylate synthesis.
- The synthesis of thymidylate (dTMP) from deoxyuridine monophosphate (dUMP) is catalyzed by thymidylate synthase. This reaction requires a reduced folate cofactor, specifically N5,N10-methylene-THF.
- N5,N10-methylene-THF is derived from N5-formyl-THF, which in turn is derived from DHF (reduced by DHFR) or THF itself.
- Inhibition of DHFR by MTX leads to a depletion of THF and its derivatives, including N5,N10-methylene-THF.
- The lack of N5,N10-methylene-THF impairs the activity of thymidylate synthase, blocking the synthesis of dTMP.
- dTMP is a necessary precursor for DNA synthesis. Impaired dTMP synthesis leads to a deficiency of thymidine, which slows down DNA replication, particularly in rapidly dividing cells like hematopoietic cells in the bone marrow.
- This impaired DNA synthesis results in megaloblastic anemia (macrocytic anemia with ineffective erythropoiesis), leukopenia, and thrombocytopenia (pancytopenia), consistent with the patient's presentation.
- The glossitis and mouth sores are also manifestations of impaired DNA synthesis in rapidly dividing epithelial cells.
- Therefore, the most direct consequence of DHFR inhibition by MTX, leading to the patient's clinical picture, is the impaired conversion of dUMP to dTMP.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) The conversion of serine to glycine. This reaction is catalyzed by serine hydroxymethyltransferase (SHMT), which uses THF as a cofactor (specifically N5,N10-methylene-THF). While THF is involved, the primary target of MTX (DHFR) is upstream of the generation of this specific THF derivative. Inhibition of DHFR affects the overall pool of THF derivatives, but the conversion of serine to glycine is not the *most direct* consequence leading to the observed megaloblastic anemia and pancytopenia.
- (C) The conversion of homocysteine to methionine. This reaction is catalyzed by methionine synthase, which requires N5-methyl-THF as a cofactor and vitamin B12. While THF is involved, and folate deficiency can cause hyperhomocysteinemia, this reaction is not the primary pathway affected by DHFR inhibition leading to impaired DNA synthesis and megaloblastic changes. The main issue is thymidylate synthesis.
- (D) The conversion of formyl-THF to methylene-THF. This reaction is catalyzed by methylene-THF reductase. While this is a step in the folate cycle and involves a THF derivative, the inhibition of DHFR occurs *before* the formation of formyl-THF from DHF. The lack of THF due to DHFR inhibition prevents the regeneration of N5,N10-methylene-THF, which is crucial for dTMP synthesis. However, the *ultimate* block leading to the clinical picture is the dTMP synthesis step itself. Option (B) describes the direct consequence of the lack of the required THF derivative.
- (E) The conversion of glutamate to glutamine. This reaction is catalyzed by glutamine synthetase, which requires ATP. It does not involve THF or folate metabolism. This option is completely unrelated to the mechanism of methotrexate or folate deficiency.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- Methotrexate inhibits dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR), depleting tetrahydrofolate (THF) derivatives, particularly N5,N10-methylene-THF, which is essential for thymidylate synthase activity. This blocks dTMP synthesis, impairs DNA replication, and causes megaloblastic anemia and pancytopenia.
- Differentials for macrocytic anemia include Vitamin B12 deficiency (which also affects homocysteine metabolism and neurological function) and other causes of impaired DNA synthesis (e.g., certain drugs like hydroxyurea, zidovudine). However, the specific mechanism of MTX toxicity involves DHFR inhibition and folate metabolism.

---

<a id='question-061'></a>

### Question 061: Dietary Folate Absorption and Metabolism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The process of dietary folate absorption in the upper jejunum, including the hydrolysis of polyglutamated folate to monoglutamated folate by the enzyme folate conjugase (gamma-glutamyl hydrolase), and the subsequent reduction and methylation steps leading to the primary circulating form, N5-methyltetrahydrofolate (N5-methyl-THF).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to his physician complaining of progressive fatigue, shortness of breath, and glossitis over the past 6 months. He reports a diet primarily consisting of processed foods and minimal intake of fresh leafy green vegetables. His past medical history is significant for long-standing, poorly controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus treated with metformin. Physical examination reveals pallor, smooth, beefy red tongue (glossitis), and mild tachycardia (110 bpm). Laboratory investigations show a mean corpuscular volume (MCV) of 115 fL (reference range: 80-100 fL), hemoglobin of 8.5 g/dL (reference range: 13.5-17.5 g/dL), and a low serum folate level of 3 ng/mL (reference range: >4 ng/mL). Red blood cell folate levels are also low at 120 ng/mL (reference range: >150 ng/mL). A peripheral blood smear shows macro-ovalocytes and hypersegmented neutrophils. The patient is started on oral folic acid supplementation (1 mg daily). After 3 months, his hematological parameters improve significantly, but his serum folate remains low at 5 ng/mL, while red blood cell folate levels normalize to 250 ng/mL. Further investigation reveals normal levels of dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR) activity in red blood cells and no detectable antibodies against intrinsic factor.

Which of the following biochemical steps is most likely impaired in this patient, explaining the persistent low serum folate despite oral folic acid supplementation and improved hematological parameters?

(A) The conversion of dihydrofolate (DHF) to tetrahydrofolate (THF) by dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR).
(B) The conversion of 5,10-methylenetetrahydrofolate to N5-methyltetrahydrofolate (N5-methyl-THF) by methylenetetrahydrofolate reductase (MTHFR).
(C) The hydrolysis of dietary polyglutamated folate to monoglutamated folate by folate conjugase (gamma-glutamyl hydrolase) in the intestinal brush border.
(D) The transport of N5-methyltetrahydrofolate (N5-methyl-THF) across the apical membrane of intestinal enterocytes via the proton-coupled folate transporter (PCFT).
(E) The conversion of N5-methyltetrahydrofolate (N5-methyl-THF) to homocysteine by methionine synthase, requiring vitamin B12 as a cofactor.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with fatigue, shortness of breath, glossitis, pallor, tachycardia. These are classic symptoms of megaloblastic anemia.
- **Dietary History:** Low intake of leafy green vegetables suggests potential folate deficiency.
- **Past Medical History:** Long-standing, poorly controlled type 2 diabetes treated with metformin. Metformin is known to interfere with vitamin B12 absorption, but the vignette specifically rules out B12 deficiency by mentioning normal DHFR activity (which requires THF, derived from folate, but B12 deficiency primarily affects methionine synthase and methylmalonyl-CoA mutase) and absence of intrinsic factor antibodies (ruling out pernicious anemia).
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - High MCV (115 fL) and low hemoglobin (8.5 g/dL) confirm macrocytic anemia.
    - Low serum folate (3 ng/mL) and low red blood cell folate (120 ng/mL) confirm folate deficiency.
    - Peripheral smear shows macro-ovalocytes and hypersegmented neutrophils, characteristic of megaloblastic anemia due to folate or B12 deficiency.
- **Response to Treatment:** Oral folic acid supplementation (1 mg daily) improves hematological parameters (implying the body can utilize the supplemented folate) but does *not* normalize serum folate levels after 3 months. Red blood cell folate levels normalize, indicating that the supplemented folate is being incorporated into red blood cells.
- **Key Deduction:** The persistent low serum folate despite adequate oral supplementation and improvement in hematological status points to a defect in the *absorption* or *initial processing* of dietary folate, rather than a defect in its utilization within cells (which would be indicated by low red cell folate despite supplementation). The fact that red cell folate normalizes suggests the supplemented folic acid (which is typically monoglutamated) is being absorbed and utilized, but the *dietary* folate is not being effectively absorbed.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Dietary Folate:** Folate in food exists primarily in polyglutamated forms (e.g., pteroylpolyglutamates).
- **Absorption:** Dietary polyglutamated folate must first be hydrolyzed to monoglutamated folate before it can be absorbed by intestinal enterocytes.
- **Enzyme:** This hydrolysis is catalyzed by the brush border enzyme folate conjugase, also known as gamma-glutamyl hydrolase. This enzyme is located on the apical surface of enterocytes in the upper jejunum.
- **Transport:** Monoglutamated folate is then transported into the enterocyte primarily via the proton-coupled folate transporter (PCFT), which is pH-dependent and saturable.
- **Intracellular Metabolism:** Inside the enterocyte, monoglutamated folate is reduced to dihydrofolate (DHF) by dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR), then to tetrahydrofolate (THF). THF is then converted through a series of reactions, including methylation, to N5-methyltetrahydrofolate (N5-methyl-THF).
- **Circulation:** N5-methyl-THF is the primary circulating form of folate in the plasma. It is transported out of the enterocyte into the portal circulation.
- **Patient's Case:** The patient has low serum folate despite adequate oral folic acid supplementation. Folic acid is a monoglutamated form. The fact that supplementation improves hematological parameters and normalizes red cell folate indicates that the enterocytes can absorb and metabolize the supplemented folic acid. However, serum folate remains low. This suggests that the patient is unable to effectively absorb dietary folate, which is primarily in the polyglutamated form. The most likely cause is impaired hydrolysis of polyglutamated folate to monoglutamated folate by folate conjugase. A deficiency or dysfunction of this enzyme would prevent the absorption of dietary folate, leading to low serum folate levels, even if supplemented folic acid is absorbed and utilized.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) The conversion of dihydrofolate (DHF) to tetrahydrofolate (THF) by dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR). Impairment of DHFR would lead to a block in folate metabolism, affecting both serum and red cell folate levels. The patient has normal DHFR activity (stated in the vignette) and responds to folic acid supplementation, ruling this out. DHFR inhibitors like methotrexate cause folate deficiency, but this patient's presentation and response don't fit.
- (B) The conversion of 5,10-methylenetetrahydrofolate to N5-methyltetrahydrofolate (N5-methyl-THF) by methylenetetrahydrofolate reductase (MTHFR). MTHFR deficiency is a common genetic polymorphism but typically does not cause severe folate deficiency or megaloblastic anemia unless other factors are present (e.g., very low folate intake, B12 deficiency). Furthermore, MTHFR acts intracellularly, and its deficiency would primarily affect the levels of N5-methyl-THF *within* cells, not necessarily serum folate levels in a way that explains the specific pattern seen here (low serum folate despite normal red cell folate after supplementation).
- (D) The transport of N5-methyltetrahydrofolate (N5-methyl-THF) across the apical membrane of intestinal enterocytes via the proton-coupled folate transporter (PCFT). PCFT transports monoglutamated folate *into* the enterocyte. While PCFT dysfunction can cause folate malabsorption, it would affect the absorption of *both* dietary (monoglutamated after hydrolysis) and supplemented (monoglutamated) folic acid. The fact that the patient responds well to folic acid supplementation argues against a primary defect in PCFT. PCFT is also involved in the efflux of N5-methyl-THF *out* of the enterocyte into the portal circulation. Impaired efflux would lead to low serum folate, but it wouldn't explain why dietary folate isn't absorbed effectively, as the initial step (hydrolysis) would still occur.
- (E) The conversion of N5-methyltetrahydrofolate (N5-methyl-THF) to homocysteine by methionine synthase, requiring vitamin B12 as a cofactor. This reaction occurs intracellularly and is primarily affected by vitamin B12 deficiency. Methionine synthase deficiency or B12 deficiency would lead to elevated homocysteine and methylmalonic acid (MMA) levels, and impaired folate utilization, affecting both serum and red cell folate. The vignette rules out B12 deficiency (normal DHFR activity, no IF antibodies). This step is downstream of folate absorption and transport.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- Dietary folate absorption requires initial hydrolysis of polyglutamated forms to monoglutamated forms by folate conjugase (gamma-glutamyl hydrolase) in the intestinal brush border.
- Impaired folate conjugase activity leads to malabsorption of dietary folate, resulting in low serum folate levels, which may not respond adequately to oral folic acid supplementation if the diet is the primary source of folate.
- Differentiate this from defects in folate transport (PCFT), intracellular metabolism (DHFR, MTHFR), or utilization (methionine synthase, B12 deficiency) based on the pattern of serum vs. red cell folate levels and response to supplementation.
- Differentials for macrocytic anemia include folate deficiency, vitamin B12 deficiency, myelodysplastic syndromes, liver disease, hypothyroidism, and certain medications (e.g., hydroxyurea, zidovudine). Always consider both folate and B12 deficiency.

<a id='question-062'></a>

### Question 062: Folate Nutritional Reserve: Modest Liver Stores Depleted Within 3 to 4 Months of Inadequate Dietary Intake
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Folate (Vitamin B9) is a water-soluble vitamin essential for one-carbon metabolism, DNA synthesis, and cell division. Unlike fat-soluble vitamins (A, D, E, K) which are stored in the liver, folate has modest liver stores that are depleted relatively quickly (within 3-4 months) with inadequate dietary intake, leading to deficiency symptoms. (叶酸（维生素B9）是一种水溶性维生素，对一碳代谢、DNA合成和细胞分裂至关重要。与储存在肝脏中的脂溶性维生素（A、D、E、K）不同，叶酸的肝脏储备量很小，在摄入不足时会迅速耗尽（3-4个月内），从而导致缺乏症状。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of chronic alcoholism and poor dietary habits presents to the clinic complaining of fatigue, shortness of breath, and glossitis (sore, red tongue). He reports consuming minimal fresh fruits and vegetables for the past 6 months. Physical examination reveals pallor, tachycardia (110 bpm), and a smooth, beefy red tongue. Laboratory results show a mean corpuscular volume (MCV) of 115 fL (normal range 80-100 fL), hemoglobin of 8.5 g/dL (normal range 13.5-17.5 g/dL for males), and a low serum folate level of 5 ng/mL (normal range > 7 ng/mL). A peripheral blood smear shows macro-ovalocytes and hypersegmented neutrophils (neutrophils with >5 lobes). Serum vitamin B12 level is within the normal range (300 pg/mL, normal range 200-900 pg/mL). The patient is started on oral folic acid supplementation. Which of the following best explains the relatively rapid onset of his hematologic abnormalities despite the presence of liver stores for some vitamins?

(A) Folate deficiency leads to impaired DNA synthesis, causing ineffective erythropoiesis and megaloblastic anemia, but the liver stores are substantial, preventing deficiency for years.
(B) Folate is a fat-soluble vitamin, readily stored in the liver's adipose tissue, providing a large reserve that takes many years to deplete.
(C) Unlike vitamin B12, folate is not stored in the body and must be continuously supplied through diet, leading to rapid deficiency symptoms.
(D) Folate has modest liver stores, primarily in the polyglutamate form, which are depleted within 3-4 months of inadequate dietary intake, leading to impaired DNA synthesis and megaloblastic anemia.
(E) The patient's alcoholism causes direct liver damage, impairing the liver's ability to store any vitamins, including folate, leading to rapid deficiency.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male, chronic alcoholism, poor diet (minimal fruits/vegetables for 6 months). These factors suggest risk for nutritional deficiencies, particularly water-soluble vitamins like folate and thiamine.
- **Symptoms:** Fatigue, shortness of breath (anemia), glossitis (sore tongue). These are classic symptoms of megaloblastic anemia.
- **Physical Exam:** Pallor (anemia), tachycardia (compensatory for anemia), smooth, beefy red tongue (glossitis).
- **Lab Findings:**
    - MCV 115 fL (High): Indicates macrocytic anemia.
    - Hemoglobin 8.5 g/dL (Low): Confirms anemia.
    - Serum Folate 5 ng/mL (Low): Directly indicates folate deficiency.
    - Peripheral Smear: Macro-ovalocytes, hypersegmented neutrophils. These are pathognomonic for megaloblastic anemia.
    - Serum B12 300 pg/mL (Normal): Rules out B12 deficiency as the primary cause of the megaloblastic anemia.
- **Treatment:** Oral folic acid supplementation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the reason behind the *relatively rapid* onset of hematologic abnormalities despite the existence of liver stores for some vitamins. This points to the specific storage characteristics of folate.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Folate Metabolism:** Folate (pteroylglutamic acid) is a water-soluble vitamin crucial for one-carbon metabolism. It exists in various forms, including tetrahydrofolate (THF), which acts as a coenzyme in reactions like the synthesis of purines, thymidylate (dTMP), and certain amino acids (methionine, serine, glycine).
- **Folate Storage:** Unlike fat-soluble vitamins (A, D, E, K) which are stored in significant amounts in the liver and adipose tissue, folate has *modest* liver stores. The primary storage form is polyglutamated folate, which is less readily absorbed than monoglutamated folate from the diet or supplements.
- **Depletion Rate:** Due to these limited stores, body reserves of folate are typically depleted within 3 to 4 months of inadequate dietary intake or increased demand (e.g., pregnancy, hemolysis).
- **Mechanism of Anemia:** Folate deficiency impairs the synthesis of thymidylate (dTMP) via the enzyme thymidylate synthase, which requires 5,10-methylenetetrahydrofolate as a cofactor. Lack of dTMP leads to impaired DNA synthesis, particularly affecting rapidly dividing cells like hematopoietic stem cells in the bone marrow. This results in ineffective erythropoiesis (premature destruction of red blood cell precursors) and megaloblastic anemia, characterized by macrocytic red blood cells (high MCV) and hypersegmented neutrophils.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's history (alcoholism, poor diet), symptoms (fatigue, SOB, glossitis), physical findings (pallor, tachycardia, glossitis), and lab results (low Hb, high MCV, low folate, megaloblastic changes on smear, normal B12) are all consistent with folate deficiency anemia. The question specifically asks why the deficiency developed relatively quickly (within 6 months of poor diet), which is explained by the limited liver stores of folate.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. Folate deficiency *does* cause impaired DNA synthesis and megaloblastic anemia. However, the statement that liver stores are substantial and prevent deficiency for years is false. Folate stores are modest and deplete relatively quickly.
- (B): Incorrect. Folate is a *water-soluble* vitamin, not fat-soluble. Fat-soluble vitamins are stored in the liver and adipose tissue in large amounts. Folate has limited storage capacity.
- (C): Incorrect. While folate is not stored in large amounts, it is stored to some extent in the liver. The statement that it is not stored *at all* is inaccurate. Vitamin B12, on the other hand, is stored in the liver for several years, which is why B12 deficiency symptoms take longer to develop.
- (E): Incorrect. While chronic alcoholism can cause liver damage, the primary reason for the rapid depletion of folate stores is the vitamin's inherent limited storage capacity, not necessarily direct impairment of storage due to liver damage (although severe liver disease can affect folate metabolism). The question focuses on the general characteristic of folate storage.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Folate is a water-soluble vitamin with modest liver stores that are depleted within 3-4 months of inadequate intake, leading to megaloblastic anemia. This contrasts with fat-soluble vitamins (stored for years) and vitamin B12 (stored for years).
- **Differentials:** Megaloblastic anemia can also be caused by vitamin B12 deficiency. Differentiating between folate and B12 deficiency often involves checking serum levels of both vitamins and potentially measuring methylmalonic acid (MMA) and homocysteine levels (both elevated in B12 deficiency, only homocysteine elevated in folate deficiency). Other causes of macrocytic anemia include liver disease, alcoholism (independent of folate), hypothyroidism, and certain medications.

<a id='question-063'></a>

### Question 063: Folate Deficiency Manifestations: Megaloblastic Macrocytic Anemia, Hypersegmented Neutrophils (>5 Lobes), and Elevated Serum Homocysteine
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Folate deficiency leads to impaired DNA synthesis, resulting in megaloblastic anemia (macrocytic RBCs, hypersegmented neutrophils) and accumulation of homocysteine due to impaired remethylation of homocysteine to methionine. (叶酸缺乏导致DNA合成受损，引起巨幼红细胞性贫血（大细胞RBCs，多叶核中性粒细胞）和同型半胱氨酸积累，因为同型半胱氨酸重甲基化为甲硫氨酸的过程受损。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of chronic alcoholism and poor dietary habits presents to the clinic complaining of fatigue, shortness of breath, and glossitis (sore tongue). Physical examination reveals pallor and a smooth, beefy-red tongue. Complete blood count (CBC) shows a mean corpuscular volume (MCV) of 115 fL (normal range: 80-100 fL), hemoglobin of 8.5 g/dL (normal range: 13.5-17.5 g/dL), and a peripheral blood smear showing macrocytic red blood cells and hypersegmented neutrophils (neutrophils with >5 nuclear lobes). Serum folate level is markedly decreased (2 ng/mL, normal range: 3-17 ng/mL). Serum vitamin B12 level is within the normal range (250 pg/mL, normal range: 200-900 pg/mL). Serum homocysteine level is elevated at 25 µmol/L (normal range: 5-15 µmol/L). Serum methylmalonic acid (MMA) level is within the normal range (0.2 µmol/L, normal range: 0.05-0.25 µmol/L). Which of the following biochemical pathways is most directly impaired by the underlying deficiency, leading to the observed elevation in serum homocysteine?

(A) Conversion of methionine to S-adenosylmethionine (SAM) by methionine adenosyltransferase.
(B) Conversion of 5-methyltetrahydrofolate (5-methyl-THF) to tetrahydrofolate (THF) by methionine synthase.
(C) Conversion of propionyl-CoA to methylmalonyl-CoA by methylmalonyl-CoA mutase.
(D) Conversion of serine and glycine to cysteine by cystathionine β-synthase.
(E) Conversion of N5,N10-methylene-THF to 5-methyl-THF by methylenetetrahydrofolate reductase (MTHFR).

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 68-year-old male, chronic alcoholism, poor diet, fatigue, shortness of breath, glossitis, pallor, beefy-red tongue. These symptoms suggest anemia and potential nutritional deficiencies, common in alcoholics.
- **CBC Findings:** MCV 115 fL (macrocytic), Hb 8.5 g/dL (anemia), peripheral smear shows macrocytic RBCs and hypersegmented neutrophils (>5 lobes). This constellation is characteristic of megaloblastic anemia.
- **Lab Findings:** Low serum folate (2 ng/mL), normal serum B12 (250 pg/mL), elevated serum homocysteine (25 µmol/L), normal serum MMA (0.2 µmol/L).
- **Deduction:** The patient has megaloblastic anemia due to folate deficiency (low folate, normal B12). Elevated homocysteine is a known consequence of folate deficiency, while normal MMA levels help differentiate it from B12 deficiency (which causes elevated MMA). The question asks for the specific biochemical pathway impaired by folate deficiency that leads to elevated homocysteine.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Folate (vitamin B9) is crucial for one-carbon metabolism, acting as a cofactor (primarily as THF derivatives) in various enzymatic reactions, including nucleotide synthesis (purines and pyrimidines) and amino acid metabolism.
- Impaired DNA synthesis due to folate deficiency leads to ineffective erythropoiesis and megaloblastic changes in rapidly dividing cells (RBC precursors, neutrophils).
- Folate deficiency specifically affects the remethylation of homocysteine to methionine. This reaction is catalyzed by methionine synthase (also known as homocysteine methyltransferase).
- Methionine synthase requires two cofactors: vitamin B12 (as methylcobalamin) and 5-methyltetrahydrofolate (5-methyl-THF).
- The reaction is: Homocysteine + 5-methyl-THF → Methionine + THF.
- In folate deficiency, there is insufficient 5-methyl-THF available to participate in this reaction. This leads to a buildup of homocysteine.
- The THF generated in this reaction is then converted back to other active folate cofactors, including 5,10-methylenetetrahydrofolate, which is needed for purine synthesis.
- The enzyme methionine synthase uses 5-methyl-THF and transfers its methyl group to homocysteine, forming methionine and regenerating THF. Therefore, the conversion of 5-methyl-THF to THF by methionine synthase is the direct step impaired due to lack of the substrate 5-methyl-THF in folate deficiency, leading to homocysteine accumulation.
- Option (B) describes this specific reaction catalyzed by methionine synthase, which is directly dependent on folate (as 5-methyl-THF) and is impaired in folate deficiency, causing homocysteine elevation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Conversion of methionine to S-adenosylmethionine (SAM) by methionine adenosyltransferase. This reaction is catalyzed by MAT and requires ATP. It is not directly dependent on folate and is not the primary cause of elevated homocysteine in folate deficiency. SAM is a major methyl group donor in many reactions.
- (C): Conversion of propionyl-CoA to methylmalonyl-CoA by methylmalonyl-CoA mutase. This reaction requires vitamin B12 (as adenosylcobalamin) as a cofactor. Deficiency of B12, not folate, impairs this step, leading to the accumulation of methylmalonic acid (MMA). The patient has normal MMA levels, ruling out B12 deficiency and making this pathway irrelevant to the elevated homocysteine in this case.
- (D): Conversion of serine and glycine to cysteine by cystathionine β-synthase. This reaction is part of the transsulfuration pathway, which converts homocysteine to cysteine. While this pathway *lowers* homocysteine, it is not directly dependent on folate in the way the methionine synthase reaction is. Impairment here would *increase* homocysteine, but it's not the primary folate-dependent step causing the elevation.
- (E): Conversion of N5,N10-methylene-THF to 5-methyl-THF by methylenetetrahydrofolate reductase (MTHFR). This reaction is catalyzed by MTHFR and is crucial for regenerating 5-methyl-THF from N5,N10-methylene-THF. While MTHFR activity is important for folate metabolism, the *deficiency* of folate itself leads to a lack of 5-methyl-THF available for the methionine synthase reaction. The question asks which pathway is *impaired* by the *deficiency*, leading to elevated homocysteine. The impairment is the lack of 5-methyl-THF *for* the methionine synthase reaction (Option B), not the MTHFR reaction itself (although MTHFR polymorphisms can affect folate status). The MTHFR reaction *produces* 5-methyl-THF, which is needed for homocysteine remethylation. In folate deficiency, the *input* (folate) is low, leading to low 5-methyl-THF, which impairs the methionine synthase reaction.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Folate deficiency causes megaloblastic anemia (macrocytic RBCs, hypersegmented neutrophils) and elevated serum homocysteine due to impaired remethylation of homocysteine to methionine by methionine synthase, which requires 5-methyl-THF. Vitamin B12 deficiency also causes megaloblastic anemia and elevated homocysteine but additionally causes elevated methylmalonic acid (MMA) due to impaired conversion of methylmalonyl-CoA to succinyl-CoA by methylmalonyl-CoA mutase. Differentiating between folate and B12 deficiency relies on serum levels of both vitamins, homocysteine, and MMA.

---

<a id='question-064'></a>

### Question 064: Methylmalonic Acid Levels in Folate Deficiency
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating folate deficiency from vitamin B12 deficiency based on specific biochemical markers, specifically methylmalonic acid (MMA) levels. (区分叶酸缺乏症与维生素B12缺乏症的关键生化指标，特别是甲基丙二酸（MMA）水平。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old woman presents to her primary care physician complaining of progressive fatigue, shortness of breath on exertion, and glossitis over the past 6 months. Her past medical history is significant for hypertension and type 2 diabetes mellitus, both managed with medication. She reports a diet primarily consisting of processed foods and limited fresh fruits and vegetables. Physical examination reveals pallor, smooth tongue (glossitis), and mild peripheral neuropathy characterized by decreased sensation in her lower extremities. Laboratory investigations show a mean corpuscular volume (MCV) of 115 fL (normal range 80-100 fL), hemoglobin of 8.5 g/dL (normal range 12-16 g/dL for women), and a low serum folate level of 5 ng/mL (normal range > 4 ng/mL). Serum vitamin B12 level is within the normal range at 250 pg/mL (normal range 200-900 pg/mL). A Schilling test is not performed. Given her macrocytic anemia and low folate level, the physician suspects folate deficiency. However, to rule out potential B12 deficiency contributing to the neurological symptoms, further biochemical tests are ordered. These tests reveal a normal serum methylmalonic acid (MMA) level of 0.2 μmol/L (normal range 0.05-0.3 μmol/L) and an elevated serum homocysteine level of 15 μmol/L (normal range 5-12 μmol/L). Which of the following best explains the normal MMA level in this patient despite the presence of macrocytic anemia and low folate?

(A) MMA levels are elevated in both folate and vitamin B12 deficiency, making it an unreliable marker for differentiation.
(B) Folate is required as a cofactor for the enzyme methionine synthase, which converts homocysteine to methionine, but it is not involved in the metabolism of methylmalonyl-CoA.
(C) Vitamin B12 is required as a cofactor for the enzyme methylmalonyl-CoA mutase, which converts methylmalonyl-CoA to succinyl-CoA, but it is not involved in the metabolism of homocysteine.
(D) The patient's neurological symptoms are likely due to her diabetes and hypertension, not a vitamin deficiency.
(E) Elevated MMA levels are specific only to vitamin B12 deficiency and are never elevated in folate deficiency.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The patient presents with symptoms (fatigue, SOB, glossitis) and signs (pallor, glossitis, neuropathy) suggestive of anemia and potential vitamin deficiency.
- Lab results show macrocytic anemia (high MCV, low Hb) and low serum folate.
- Serum B12 is normal.
- The key finding is a *normal* serum MMA level despite macrocytic anemia and low folate.
- The question asks for the biochemical explanation for the normal MMA level in the context of folate deficiency.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Folate Deficiency:** Folate (tetrahydrofolate, THF) is essential for one-carbon metabolism. It acts as a cofactor for several enzymes, including thymidylate synthase (dTMP synthesis) and methionine synthase. Deficiency leads to impaired DNA synthesis (causing macrocytosis) and impaired methionine synthesis.
- **Vitamin B12 Deficiency:** Vitamin B12 (cobalamin) is also crucial for one-carbon metabolism. It acts as a cofactor for two key enzymes: methionine synthase and methylmalonyl-CoA mutase. Deficiency leads to impaired methionine synthesis (causing macrocytosis and elevated homocysteine) and impaired breakdown of methylmalonyl-CoA (causing elevated MMA).
- **Methionine Synthase:** This enzyme catalyzes the conversion of homocysteine to methionine, using methylcobalamin (a form of B12) as a cofactor and N5-methyltetrahydrofolate (N5-methyl-THF) as the methyl group donor. In folate deficiency, N5-methyl-THF accumulates because it cannot be converted to THF, which is needed for other folate-dependent reactions. However, methionine synthase activity is still dependent on B12.
- **Methylmalonyl-CoA Mutase:** This enzyme catalyzes the isomerization of L-methylmalonyl-CoA to succinyl-CoA, using adenosylcobalamin (another form of B12) as a cofactor. This reaction is part of the catabolism of odd-chain fatty acids, branched-chain amino acids (valine, isoleucine), and methionine.
- **MMA and Homocysteine Levels:**
    - In *folate deficiency*, methionine synthase activity is impaired due to the lack of N5-methyl-THF (the methyl group donor), leading to the accumulation of homocysteine. However, the methylmalonyl-CoA mutase reaction is *not* affected because it does not require folate. Therefore, MMA levels remain normal.
    - In *vitamin B12 deficiency*, both methionine synthase and methylmalonyl-CoA mutase activities are impaired due to the lack of the B12 cofactor. This leads to the accumulation of both homocysteine and MMA.
- **Patient's Case:** The patient has low folate, macrocytic anemia, and elevated homocysteine (consistent with impaired methionine synthase activity due to lack of N5-methyl-THF). However, her MMA level is normal because the methylmalonyl-CoA mutase reaction, which requires B12 but not folate, is functioning normally. This pattern (high homocysteine, normal MMA) is characteristic of isolated folate deficiency.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): This is incorrect. MMA levels are specifically elevated in B12 deficiency but typically normal in folate deficiency. This difference is a key diagnostic tool.
- (C): This statement correctly describes the role of B12 in methylmalonyl-CoA mutase but incorrectly implies B12 is *not* involved in homocysteine metabolism. B12 *is* required for methionine synthase, which converts homocysteine to methionine. This option confuses the roles of B12 in the two pathways.
- (D): This is incorrect. While diabetes and hypertension can cause neuropathy, the patient's presentation (macrocytic anemia, glossitis, low folate) strongly points towards a nutritional deficiency, specifically folate deficiency, as the primary cause of her symptoms. Attributing the neuropathy solely to other conditions ignores the significant findings related to vitamin deficiency.
- (E): This is incorrect. Elevated MMA is characteristic of B12 deficiency, not folate deficiency. In folate deficiency, MMA levels are typically normal.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- High-yield takeaway: Differentiating folate and B12 deficiency relies on specific biochemical markers. Elevated homocysteine occurs in both, but elevated MMA is specific to B12 deficiency. Normal MMA with elevated homocysteine points to folate deficiency.
- Differentials: Macrocytic anemia can be caused by folate deficiency, B12 deficiency, hypothyroidism, liver disease, myelodysplastic syndromes, and certain medications. However, the combination of low folate, normal B12, elevated homocysteine, and normal MMA strongly isolates folate deficiency as the cause in this patient.

<a id='question-065'></a>

### Question 065: Neural Tube Defects (Spina Bifida, Anencephaly)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Periconceptional maternal folate deficiency leads to impaired DNA synthesis and methylation, crucial for rapid cell division during neural tube closure, resulting in neural tube defects (NTDs) like spina bifida and anencephaly. Folate supplementation and food fortification are preventative measures. (围受孕期母亲叶酸缺乏导致DNA合成和甲基化受损，影响神经管闭合，导致神经管缺陷（NTDs），如脊髓膨出和无脑畸形。叶酸补充和食物强化是预防措施。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old primigravida presents for her first prenatal visit at 8 weeks gestation. She reports a history of irregular menses and no prior history of neural tube defect (NTD) screening or supplementation. Her diet consists primarily of processed foods, and she does not take prenatal vitamins. During the physical examination, her vital signs are within normal limits. Laboratory investigations reveal a normal complete blood count (CBC) and comprehensive metabolic panel (CMP). However, her serum folate level is significantly low at 3 ng/mL (normal range: >4 ng/mL). Ultrasound performed at 18 weeks gestation reveals a fetus with spina bifida aperta, characterized by an open defect in the lumbar spine with protrusion of the spinal cord and meninges. Karyotyping of the fetal cells obtained via amniocentesis is normal (46,XY). The parents are counseled regarding the diagnosis and management options. Given the mother's low folate level and the fetal diagnosis, which of the following biochemical mechanisms is most likely impaired during early embryonic development, leading to this condition?

(A) Impaired synthesis of purine nucleotides due to reduced availability of tetrahydrofolate (THF) derivatives required for formylation reactions.
(B) Defective DNA methylation due to decreased levels of S-adenosylmethionine (SAM), resulting from impaired folate-dependent methionine synthase activity.
(C) Reduced activity of thymidylate synthase, leading to decreased synthesis of deoxythymidine monophosphate (dTMP) and subsequent DNA strand breaks.
(D) Impaired serine hydroxymethyltransferase (SHMT) activity, causing accumulation of glycine and decreased production of 5,10-methylenetetrahydrofolate.
(E) Decreased activity of dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR), leading to reduced conversion of dihydrofolate (DHF) to tetrahydrofolate (THF) and subsequent impairment of folate-dependent reactions.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 28-year-old primigravida, 8 weeks gestation, no prior NTD screening/supplementation, poor diet, no prenatal vitamins. This profile suggests a risk factor for periconceptional folate deficiency.
- **Lab Finding:** Low serum folate (3 ng/mL). This confirms folate deficiency.
- **Fetal Diagnosis:** Spina bifida aperta at 18 weeks. This is a classic neural tube defect (NTD).
- **Genetic Finding:** Normal fetal karyotype (46,XY). This rules out chromosomal abnormalities as the primary cause.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the biochemical mechanism impaired due to maternal folate deficiency leading to NTDs.

The combination of maternal folate deficiency and fetal NTD strongly points to a defect in folate-dependent metabolic pathways crucial for DNA synthesis during rapid cell proliferation in early embryogenesis, particularly neural tube closure (around weeks 3-4).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Folate's Role:** Folate (vitamin B9) is essential for one-carbon metabolism. Its active form, tetrahydrofolate (THF), acts as a coenzyme in various reactions, including the synthesis of purines, thymidylate (dTMP), and certain amino acids (methionine, serine, glycine).
- **Neural Tube Closure:** This process involves rapid cell division and migration of neural crest cells. DNA synthesis is critical for this rapid proliferation.
- **Folate Deficiency & DNA Synthesis:** Folate deficiency impairs the regeneration of THF from DHF (by DHFR) and the utilization of THF derivatives in key metabolic pathways.
- **Thymidylate Synthase (TS):** This enzyme catalyzes the methylation of deoxyuridine monophosphate (dUMP) to deoxythymidine monophosphate (dTMP), using 5,10-methylenetetrahydrofolate (a THF derivative) as the methyl group donor and regenerating DHF. dTMP is essential for DNA synthesis.
- **Mechanism:** In folate deficiency, the regeneration of THF is impaired (due to reduced DHFR activity or lack of THF derivatives), and the supply of 5,10-methylenetetrahydrofolate is limited. This directly inhibits thymidylate synthase activity. Reduced TS activity leads to decreased dTMP synthesis. The lack of dTMP causes "thymineless death" – an inability to synthesize DNA, leading to DNA strand breaks, impaired DNA replication, and ultimately, cell death or developmental defects, especially in rapidly dividing cells like those forming the neural tube.
- **Other Folate-Dependent Reactions:**
    - **Purine Synthesis:** THF derivatives (e.g., formyl-THF) are needed for the synthesis of purine bases (adenine, guanine). Deficiency impairs purine synthesis, but the effect on DNA synthesis via dTMP is generally considered more critical for NTDs.
    - **Methionine Synthesis:** THF derivatives are involved in the conversion of homocysteine to methionine via methionine synthase, which requires vitamin B12 as a cofactor. Deficiency impairs methionine synthesis, leading to decreased SAM production (SAM is needed for methylation reactions, including DNA methylation). While important, the direct impact on dTMP synthesis is more directly linked to the failure of DNA replication during neural tube closure.
    - **Serine Hydroxymethyltransferase (SHMT):** This enzyme converts serine and THF to glycine and 5,10-methylenetetrahydrofolate. Deficiency would impair glycine synthesis and reduce the pool of 5,10-methylenetetrahydrofolate, indirectly affecting thymidylate synthesis and purine synthesis.
    - **Dihydrofolate Reductase (DHFR):** This enzyme reduces DHF to THF. Deficiency would limit the pool of active THF coenzymes, impairing all folate-dependent reactions, including thymidylate synthesis. However, the *direct* consequence of impaired folate availability on the *specific* enzyme responsible for dTMP synthesis (TS) is the most direct link to the observed DNA synthesis defect.

Therefore, the most direct and critical biochemical consequence of folate deficiency leading to impaired DNA synthesis and NTDs is the reduced activity of thymidylate synthase due to lack of its cofactor, 5,10-methylenetetrahydrofolate.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired synthesis of purine nucleotides. While folate deficiency *does* impair purine synthesis (THF derivatives are required for formylation steps), the effect on thymidylate synthesis (dTMP) is considered the primary defect leading to the failure of DNA replication and cell division during neural tube closure. Purine deficiency is less directly implicated in NTDs compared to thymine deficiency.
- (B): Defective DNA methylation due to decreased levels of S-adenosylmethionine (SAM). Folate deficiency impairs methionine synthesis (via methionine synthase, which uses THF derivatives and B12), leading to decreased SAM. SAM is the primary methyl donor for DNA methylation. While DNA methylation is crucial for gene regulation and development, the primary defect in NTDs due to folate deficiency is the failure of *DNA replication* (due to lack of dTMP), not primarily DNA methylation. Although SAM levels are affected, the direct block in dTMP synthesis is the more critical immediate consequence for rapidly dividing cells.
- (D): Impaired serine hydroxymethyltransferase (SHMT) activity. SHMT converts serine + THF -> glycine + 5,10-methylenetetrahydrofolate. Impaired SHMT would reduce the pool of 5,10-methylenetetrahydrofolate, which is needed for thymidylate synthesis. However, the primary limitation in folate deficiency is the overall availability of THF derivatives, directly impacting TS. Impaired SHMT is not the *primary* mechanism caused by *folate deficiency itself* (though it can be affected indirectly), and the direct effect on TS is more central.
- (E): Decreased activity of dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR). DHFR converts DHF to THF. Folate deficiency *does* lead to reduced regeneration of THF, effectively decreasing DHFR activity *in situ* due to substrate limitation (lack of folate). This impairs all folate-dependent reactions. However, option (C) describes the *downstream consequence* of this THF limitation – the specific impairment of thymidylate synthase, which is the most critical enzyme for DNA synthesis affected by the lack of THF derivatives. Option (C) is a more specific and direct explanation for the failure of DNA replication leading to NTDs.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Folate deficiency during early pregnancy impairs DNA synthesis, primarily by limiting the availability of deoxythymidine monophosphate (dTMP) due to reduced thymidylate synthase activity. This failure of DNA replication is critical during neural tube closure. Key differentials include maternal diabetes (increased risk), certain medications (e.g., valproate, methotrexate), and genetic factors, but the biochemical mechanism directly linked to folate deficiency involves impaired dTMP synthesis.

<a id='question-066'></a>

### Question 066: Vitamin B12 (Cobalamin) Metabolism & Deficiency
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the unique structure of Vitamin B12 (cobalamin), its exclusive microbial synthesis, its absorption pathway involving intrinsic factor, and the biochemical consequences of deficiency, specifically affecting methionine synthase and methylmalonyl-CoA mutase. (理解维生素B12 (钴胺) 的独特结构，其排他性微生物合成，其涉及内因子吸收途径，以及缺乏症的生化后果，特别是影响甲硫氨酸合成酶和甲基丙二酰辅酶A变位酶。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of progressive numbness and tingling in his hands and feet, difficulty walking, and increasing forgetfulness over the past 6 months. His past medical history is significant for chronic atrophic gastritis, treated with proton pump inhibitors (PPIs) for the past 10 years. Family history is unremarkable. Physical examination reveals pallor, glossitis (smooth, red tongue), and impaired proprioception and vibration sense in the lower extremities. Neurological examination shows signs of upper motor neuron involvement, including hyperreflexia and a positive Babinski sign. Laboratory investigations show a mean corpuscular volume (MCV) of 115 fL (normal 80-100 fL), serum homocysteine level of 25 µmol/L (normal <15 µmol/L), and serum methylmalonic acid (MMA) level of 1.2 µmol/L (normal <0.3 µmol/L). A Schilling test reveals decreased urinary excretion of radiolabeled Vitamin B12 after oral administration, which is corrected upon co-administration with intrinsic factor. Which of the following biochemical mechanisms BEST explains the elevated serum methylmalonic acid level in this patient?

(A) Impaired activity of succinyl-CoA synthetase due to lack of biotin cofactor.
(B) Defective synthesis of the corrin ring structure of cobalamin by gut bacteria.
(C) Reduced activity of methionine synthase due to lack of cobalamin cofactor.
(D) Impaired activity of methylmalonyl-CoA mutase due to lack of cobalamin cofactor.
(E) Increased degradation of cobalamin by bacterial overgrowth in the small intestine.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with progressive neurological symptoms (numbness, tingling, gait difficulty, forgetfulness), glossitis, and impaired proprioception/vibration sense. These are classic signs of Vitamin B12 deficiency affecting the nervous system and hematopoietic system (macrocytic anemia indicated by high MCV).
*   **Past Medical History:** Chronic atrophic gastritis and long-term PPI use. Atrophic gastritis destroys parietal cells, which produce intrinsic factor (IF) necessary for B12 absorption in the terminal ileum. PPIs further impair B12 absorption by reducing gastric acid, which is needed to release B12 from food proteins.
*   **Laboratory Findings:**
    *   High MCV (115 fL): Indicates macrocytic anemia, common in B12 and folate deficiency.
    *   High serum homocysteine (25 µmol/L): B12 is a cofactor for methionine synthase, which converts homocysteine to methionine. Deficiency leads to homocysteine accumulation.
    *   High serum methylmalonic acid (MMA) (1.2 µmol/L): B12 (specifically adenosylcobalamin) is a cofactor for methylmalonyl-CoA mutase, which converts methylmalonyl-CoA to succinyl-CoA. Deficiency leads to MMA accumulation. MMA elevation is specific to B12 deficiency (unlike homocysteine, which is also elevated in folate deficiency).
*   **Schilling Test:** Decreased urinary excretion of radiolabeled B12 after oral administration, corrected by intrinsic factor co-administration. This confirms impaired B12 absorption due to lack of intrinsic factor, consistent with pernicious anemia secondary to atrophic gastritis.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the biochemical mechanism explaining the elevated serum MMA level.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Vitamin B12 (Cobalamin):** A complex molecule containing a corrin ring (similar to porphyrin but with one less pyrrole ring and a direct cobalt-nitrogen bond) and a central cobalt ion. It is synthesized exclusively by microorganisms (bacteria, archaea).
*   **Absorption:** Dietary B12 is released from food proteins by gastric acid and pepsin. It binds to R-protein (haptocorrin) in saliva and gastric juice. In the duodenum, pancreatic proteases degrade R-protein, and B12 binds to intrinsic factor (IF), produced by gastric parietal cells. The B12-IF complex travels to the terminal ileum, where it binds to specific receptors (cubilin) on enterocytes, allowing endocytosis. Inside the cell, B12 is released from IF, binds to transcobalamin II (TCII), and enters the portal circulation.
*   **Metabolic Roles:** B12 serves as a cofactor for two key human enzymes:
    1.  **Methionine Synthase:** Located in the cytoplasm. Catalyzes the transfer of a methyl group from 5-methyltetrahydrofolate (5-methylTHF) to homocysteine, forming methionine and tetrahydrofolate (THF). Requires methylcobalamin. Deficiency leads to elevated homocysteine and accumulation of 5-methylTHF (the "methyl trap").
    2.  **Methylmalonyl-CoA Mutase:** Located in the mitochondria. Catalyzes the isomerization of methylmalonyl-CoA to succinyl-CoA, a crucial step in the catabolism of odd-chain fatty acids and certain amino acids (valine, isoleucine, methionine, threonine). Requires adenosylcobalamin. Deficiency leads to elevated methylmalonyl-CoA and its precursor, methylmalonic acid (MMA).
*   **Patient's Case:** The patient has B12 deficiency due to impaired absorption (atrophic gastritis, PPI use, confirmed by Schilling test). This deficiency affects both methionine synthase and methylmalonyl-CoA mutase. The elevated MMA level specifically points to the impaired function of methylmalonyl-CoA mutase. The lack of the adenosylcobalamin cofactor prevents the conversion of methylmalonyl-CoA to succinyl-CoA, leading to MMA accumulation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Impaired activity of succinyl-CoA synthetase due to lack of biotin cofactor.** Succinyl-CoA synthetase is involved in the citric acid cycle and fatty acid synthesis/degradation, converting succinyl-CoA to succinate (or vice versa). It requires GDP or ADP as a phosphate acceptor and is *not* dependent on biotin or Vitamin B12. This option is incorrect and relates to a different enzyme and cofactor.
*   **(B) Defective synthesis of the corrin ring structure of cobalamin by gut bacteria.** While gut bacteria synthesize cobalamin, humans cannot absorb it directly from bacteria. Furthermore, the *patient* does not have a defect in synthesizing the corrin ring; they have a defect in *absorbing* pre-formed cobalamin from the diet. The synthesis by bacteria is irrelevant to the patient's deficiency mechanism. This option is incorrect.
*   **(C) Reduced activity of methionine synthase due to lack of cobalamin cofactor.** This is true – B12 deficiency *does* reduce methionine synthase activity, leading to elevated homocysteine. However, the question specifically asks for the mechanism explaining the elevated *methylmalonic acid* level. Methionine synthase deficiency explains the elevated homocysteine, not the elevated MMA. This option describes a consequence of B12 deficiency but not the cause of elevated MMA.
*   **(E) Increased degradation of cobalamin by bacterial overgrowth in the small intestine.** Bacterial overgrowth can *compete* for dietary B12, potentially worsening deficiency, but it does not directly explain the elevated MMA level. The primary mechanism for elevated MMA is the lack of the B12 cofactor for methylmalonyl-CoA mutase. While bacterial overgrowth might contribute to the overall B12 deficiency, it's not the direct biochemical explanation for the elevated MMA. This option is incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Vitamin B12 deficiency leads to impaired function of two key enzymes: methionine synthase (causing elevated homocysteine) and methylmalonyl-CoA mutase (causing elevated methylmalonic acid). Elevated MMA is specific to B12 deficiency, distinguishing it from folate deficiency (which causes elevated homocysteine but normal MMA). Remember the two enzymes and their respective substrates/products. Key differentials for macrocytic anemia include B12 deficiency, folate deficiency, myelodysplastic syndromes, liver disease, hypothyroidism, and certain medications.

---

<a id='question-067'></a>

### Question 067: Vitamin B12 Absorption Physiology
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the sequential steps of vitamin B12 absorption in the gastrointestinal tract, including binding to haptocorrin (R-binder) in the stomach, cleavage by pancreatic proteases in the duodenum, and subsequent binding to intrinsic factor (IF) produced by gastric parietal cells for absorption in the ileum. (理解维生素B12在胃肠道吸收的顺序步骤，包括胃中与转钴胺素（R-结合蛋白）结合、十二指肠中被胰腺蛋白酶切割、以及随后与由胃壁细胞产生的内因子结合并在回肠吸收。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to his physician complaining of progressive fatigue, shortness of breath, and numbness/tingling in his hands and feet over the past 6 months. His past medical history is significant for chronic atrophic gastritis, which was diagnosed 5 years ago following a *Helicobacter pylori* infection. He denies any recent dietary changes or supplement use. Physical examination reveals pallor, glossitis, and diminished vibratory sensation in the lower extremities. Laboratory studies show a mean corpuscular volume (MCV) of 115 fL (normal 80-100 fL), serum vitamin B12 level of 80 pg/mL (normal 200-900 pg/mL), elevated serum methylmalonic acid (MMA) and homocysteine levels, and positive anti-parietal cell antibodies. A Schilling test is performed. In the first stage, the patient ingests 1 μg of radioactive vitamin B12 orally, followed by an intramuscular injection of 1000 μg of non-radioactive vitamin B12. Two hours later, only 2% of the administered radioactive vitamin B12 is excreted in the urine. In the second stage, the patient ingests 1 μg of radioactive vitamin B12 orally along with 100 μg of purified human intrinsic factor. Two hours later, 35% of the administered radioactive vitamin B12 is excreted in the urine. Based on these findings, which of the following best describes the primary defect in this patient's vitamin B12 absorption?

(A) Impaired binding of vitamin B12 to haptocorrin in the stomach.
(B) Impaired cleavage of the vitamin B12-haptocorrin complex by pancreatic proteases.
(C) Impaired secretion of intrinsic factor by gastric parietal cells.
(D) Impaired binding of the vitamin B12-intrinsic factor complex to receptors in the ileum.
(E) Impaired transport of the vitamin B12-intrinsic factor complex across the apical membrane of ileal enterocytes.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation**: 62-year-old male with fatigue, shortness of breath, paresthesias (neurological symptoms), pallor, glossitis (signs of megaloblastic anemia).
- **Past Medical History**: Chronic atrophic gastritis (autoimmune or *H. pylori*-induced) is a key risk factor for pernicious anemia due to destruction of parietal cells.
- **Laboratory Findings**: High MCV (macrocytosis), low serum B12, elevated MMA and homocysteine (metabolic consequences of B12 deficiency), positive anti-parietal cell antibodies (suggests autoimmune gastritis). These strongly point towards vitamin B12 deficiency causing megaloblastic anemia.
- **Schilling Test - Stage 1**: Oral radioactive B12 + IM non-radioactive B12. Only 2% excreted in urine. This indicates that a significant amount of the ingested B12 is being absorbed, but the amount excreted is low. The IM B12 saturates plasma binding proteins, forcing any absorbed radioactive B12 into the urine. The low excretion suggests either impaired absorption *or* impaired release of absorbed B12 from enterocytes back into circulation (enterohepatic circulation). However, the very low percentage suggests a significant absorption defect is present.
- **Schilling Test - Stage 2**: Oral radioactive B12 + oral intrinsic factor (IF). 35% excreted in urine. This is a significant increase compared to Stage 1 (2%). This indicates that providing exogenous IF corrects or significantly improves the absorption of B12.
- **Deduction**: The patient has clinical and biochemical evidence of B12 deficiency. The Schilling test shows impaired B12 absorption in Stage 1, which is corrected by adding IF in Stage 2. This pattern is characteristic of a lack of intrinsic factor. The patient's history of atrophic gastritis and positive anti-parietal cell antibodies strongly support the diagnosis of pernicious anemia, where autoimmune destruction of gastric parietal cells leads to IF deficiency.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Vitamin B12 Absorption Pathway**:
    1.  **Stomach**: Dietary B12 (cobalamin) is released from food proteins by pepsin and gastric acid. It then binds to R-binder (haptocorrin), a protein secreted in saliva and gastric juice. This binding protects B12 from degradation in the acidic environment.
    2.  **Duodenum**: The B12-R-binder complex travels to the duodenum. Here, pancreatic proteases (like trypsin) cleave R-binder, releasing B12.
    3.  **Duodenum/Proximal Jejunum**: Free B12 then binds to intrinsic factor (IF), a glycoprotein secreted by gastric parietal cells. This binding is crucial for subsequent absorption.
    4.  **Ileum (Terminal)**: The B12-IF complex travels to the terminal ileum, where it binds to specific receptors (cubilin) on the apical surface of enterocytes.
    5.  **Enterocyte Uptake**: The B12-IF complex is internalized via receptor-mediated endocytosis.
    6.  **Lysosomal Release**: Within the enterocyte, B12 is released from IF in lysosomes (acidic environment).
    7.  **Binding to Transcobalamin II**: B12 binds to transcobalamin II (TCII), a transport protein.
    8.  **Basolateral Transport**: The B12-TCII complex is transported across the basolateral membrane into the portal circulation.
    9.  **Liver Uptake**: The liver takes up most of the B12-TCII complex.
- **Schilling Test Interpretation**:
    - Stage 1 (Oral B12 alone): Measures overall B12 absorption. Low excretion (<10%) indicates impaired absorption.
    - Stage 2 (Oral B12 + IF): Measures absorption if IF is the limiting factor. If excretion increases significantly (>25-30%), it indicates IF deficiency is the cause of impaired absorption.
    - Stage 3 (Oral B12 + IF + antibiotics): Used to check for bacterial overgrowth in the small intestine, which can consume B12.
    - Stage 4 (Oral B12 + pancreatic enzymes): Used to check for pancreatic insufficiency, which impairs R-binder cleavage.
- **Patient's Case**: The patient has atrophic gastritis (destruction of parietal cells) and positive anti-parietal cell antibodies, indicating pernicious anemia. The Schilling test results (Stage 1 low excretion, Stage 2 high excretion) confirm that the primary defect is the lack of intrinsic factor.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Impaired binding of vitamin B12 to haptocorrin in the stomach. Incorrect. B12 binds readily to haptocorrin in the stomach. A defect here would likely lead to B12 degradation in the acidic environment, but the Schilling test pattern (correction with IF) doesn't fit. Also, haptocorrin binding is generally efficient.
- (B) Impaired cleavage of the vitamin B12-haptocorrin complex by pancreatic proteases. Incorrect. This would prevent B12 from binding to IF. The Schilling test would show impaired absorption in Stage 1, but adding IF in Stage 2 would *not* correct the absorption, as B12 would still be bound to R-binder and unable to bind IF. The patient's Stage 2 result shows correction with IF, ruling this out.
- (D) Impaired binding of the vitamin B12-intrinsic factor complex to receptors in the ileum. Incorrect. This would impair the final uptake step in the ileum. The Schilling test would show impaired absorption in Stage 1, and adding IF in Stage 2 would *not* correct the absorption because the B12-IF complex would still form but fail to bind the ileal receptor. The patient's Stage 2 result shows correction with IF, ruling this out. This scenario might be seen in Imerslund-Gräsbeck syndrome (defective cubilin receptor).
- (E) Impaired transport of the vitamin B12-intrinsic factor complex across the apical membrane of ileal enterocytes. Incorrect. This refers to the receptor-mediated endocytosis step. Similar to option (D), impaired transport *after* IF binding would mean that adding IF in Stage 2 would not correct the absorption defect shown in Stage 1. The patient's Stage 2 result shows correction with IF, ruling this out.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The sequential steps of B12 absorption (stomach: B12-R-binder; duodenum: R-binder cleavage, B12-IF formation; ileum: B12-IF receptor binding/uptake) must be understood. The Schilling test is crucial for diagnosing B12 malabsorption and differentiating between causes like pernicious anemia (IF deficiency), pancreatic insufficiency (R-binder cleavage defect), bacterial overgrowth, or ileal disease (receptor/transport defect). Pernicious anemia, often autoimmune, is characterized by destruction of gastric parietal cells, leading to IF deficiency. Key differentials include dietary deficiency (rare), malabsorption syndromes (Crohn's disease affecting the ileum), and certain medications (e.g., metformin, PPIs).

---

<a id='question-068'></a>

### Question 068: Cobalamin Deficiency
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The absorption of vitamin B12 (cobalamin) requires intrinsic factor (IF) produced by gastric parietal cells, followed by binding of the IF-cobalamin complex to the cubilin receptor (Cubam complex) in the terminal ileum for receptor-mediated endocytosis. Defects in this pathway lead to pernicious anemia or other forms of cobalamin deficiency. (维生素B12 (钴胺素) 的吸收需要胃壁细胞产生的内因子 (IF)，然后内因子-钴胺素复合物与回肠末端的古巴受体 (Cubam 复合物) 结合进行受体介导的内吞作用。该途径的缺陷会导致恶性贫血或其他形式的钴胺素缺乏。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of progressive fatigue, shortness of breath, and numbness/tingling in his hands and feet over the past 6 months. His past medical history is significant for autoimmune gastritis, diagnosed 5 years ago, which led to chronic atrophic gastritis and subsequent gastric resection. He denies alcohol abuse or significant dietary restrictions. Physical examination reveals pallor, glossitis (smooth, red tongue), and diminished vibratory sensation in the lower extremities. Laboratory findings show a mean corpuscular volume (MCV) of 115 fL (normal 80-100 fL), serum cobalamin level of 80 pg/mL (normal 200-900 pg/mL), elevated serum methylmalonic acid (MMA) level of 4.5 µmol/L (normal <0.3 µmol/L), elevated serum homocysteine level of 25 µmol/L (normal <15 µmol/L), and positive anti-parietal cell antibodies. A Schilling test (Stage I, oral radiolabeled cobalamin without IF) shows <5% urinary excretion of the radiolabeled cobalamin. A subsequent Schilling test (Stage II, oral radiolabeled cobalamin with added IF) shows 20% urinary excretion.

What is the most likely underlying mechanism responsible for this patient's cobalamin deficiency?

(A) Impaired transport of cobalamin across the apical membrane of ileal enterocytes due to a defect in the proton-coupled folate transporter (PCFT).
(B) Reduced synthesis of intrinsic factor (IF) due to autoimmune destruction of gastric parietal cells.
(C) Defective receptor-mediated endocytosis of the IF-cobalamin complex in the terminal ileum due to mutations in the *CUBN* gene encoding cubilin.
(D) Impaired lysosomal degradation of the IF-cobalamin complex within ileal enterocytes due to a defect in the Niemann-Pick C1 (NPC1) protein.
(E) Inadequate dietary intake of cobalamin leading to depletion of hepatic stores.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with fatigue, shortness of breath, paresthesias (numbness/tingling), pallor, glossitis, diminished vibratory sensation. These symptoms are classic for megaloblastic anemia, often caused by vitamin B12 or folate deficiency. The neurological symptoms (paresthesias, diminished vibration sense) strongly point towards B12 deficiency.
- **Past Medical History:** Autoimmune gastritis leading to chronic atrophic gastritis and gastric resection. Autoimmune gastritis specifically targets gastric parietal cells, which are responsible for producing intrinsic factor (IF) and hydrochloric acid. Gastric resection further removes the source of IF. This history is a major clue pointing towards impaired IF production.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - MCV 115 fL: Elevated MCV indicates macrocytic anemia, consistent with megaloblastic anemia.
    - Serum cobalamin 80 pg/mL: Low cobalamin level confirms B12 deficiency.
    - Elevated MMA and homocysteine: Both MMA and homocysteine levels are elevated. Elevated MMA is specific for B12 deficiency (as B12 is a cofactor for methylmalonyl-CoA mutase), while elevated homocysteine can occur in both B12 and folate deficiency (B12 is a cofactor for methionine synthase). The combination strongly supports B12 deficiency.
    - Positive anti-parietal cell antibodies: Confirms the autoimmune nature of the gastritis, targeting the parietal cells that produce IF.
- **Schilling Test Results:**
    - Stage I (<5% excretion): Oral radiolabeled cobalamin is poorly absorbed, indicating a problem either with IF production/availability or ileal absorption.
    - Stage II (20% excretion): Adding exogenous IF improves cobalamin absorption significantly (from <5% to 20%). This indicates that the ileal absorption machinery (receptors) is functional, but the endogenous IF is deficient.
- **Conclusion:** The clinical picture (megaloblastic anemia with neurological symptoms), history (autoimmune gastritis, gastric resection), lab findings (low B12, high MMA/homocysteine, anti-parietal cell antibodies), and Schilling test results (poor absorption without IF, improved absorption with added IF) all converge on a deficiency of intrinsic factor (IF) as the primary cause of the cobalamin malabsorption.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Cobalamin Absorption:** Dietary cobalamin is released from food proteins by gastric acid and pepsin. It then binds to R-protein (haptocorrin) secreted by salivary glands and gastric mucosa. In the duodenum, pancreatic proteases degrade R-protein, releasing cobalamin. Free cobalamin then binds to intrinsic factor (IF), a glycoprotein secreted by gastric parietal cells.
- **IF-Cobalamin Complex:** The IF-cobalamin complex travels to the terminal ileum.
- **Ileal Absorption:** In the terminal ileum, the IF-cobalamin complex binds to a specific receptor complex located on the apical surface of enterocytes. This receptor complex consists of cubilin (encoded by *CUBN*) and amnionless (encoded by *AMNL*). This binding triggers receptor-mediated endocytosis of the complex.
- **Intracellular Processing:** Inside the enterocyte, the IF-cobalamin complex is processed within lysosomes. IF is degraded, and cobalamin is released.
- **Transport to Circulation:** Cobalamin is then bound to transcobalamin II (TCII) and transported across the basolateral membrane into the portal circulation.
- **Patient's Case:** This patient has autoimmune gastritis leading to destruction of parietal cells and subsequent gastric resection. Parietal cells produce IF. Therefore, the lack of functional parietal cells results in insufficient IF production. The Schilling test confirms this: adding exogenous IF bypasses the endogenous deficiency and allows for significant absorption (Stage II improvement). The underlying mechanism is the lack of IF, not a defect in ileal absorption (which would not improve significantly with added IF).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired transport across the apical membrane due to PCFT defect. PCFT (proton-coupled folate transporter) is involved in folate absorption, not cobalamin absorption. Cobalamin absorption involves the Cubam complex (cubilin/amnionless). This option confuses folate and cobalamin transport mechanisms.
- (C): Defective receptor-mediated endocytosis due to *CUBN* mutations. This describes Imerslund-Gräsbeck syndrome (an autosomal recessive disorder) or other rare causes of ileal cobalamin malabsorption. While this *is* a mechanism of cobalamin deficiency, the patient's history of autoimmune gastritis, gastric resection, and the Schilling test results (improvement with added IF) strongly point to IF deficiency, not a primary ileal receptor defect. If the ileal receptor were defective, adding IF would not significantly improve absorption in the Schilling test Stage II.
- (D): Impaired lysosomal degradation due to NPC1 defect. Niemann-Pick disease type C (NPC) is a lysosomal storage disorder caused by mutations in *NPC1* or *NPC2*. It can cause megaloblastic anemia due to impaired intracellular trafficking and degradation of the IF-cobalamin complex within enterocytes. However, this is a rare condition, and the patient's history strongly suggests autoimmune gastritis and IF deficiency as the primary cause. The Schilling test results are also more consistent with IF deficiency than a lysosomal degradation defect (though both might show some improvement with added IF, the degree of improvement and the specific clinical context favor IF deficiency).
- (E): Inadequate dietary intake. While dietary deficiency can cause B12 deficiency, it is rare in developed countries unless the patient is a strict vegan for many years or has severe malabsorption syndromes. This patient's history points to a specific malabsorption mechanism related to IF, not simple dietary insufficiency. Furthermore, the Schilling test results clearly indicate a problem with IF availability or ileal absorption, not just inadequate intake.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The absorption of vitamin B12 requires intrinsic factor (IF) produced by gastric parietal cells and subsequent binding to the cubilin receptor complex in the terminal ileum. Autoimmune gastritis destroys parietal cells, leading to IF deficiency and pernicious anemia. The Schilling test helps differentiate between IF deficiency (improves with added IF) and ileal malabsorption (does not improve significantly with added IF). Key differentials for B12 deficiency include dietary insufficiency (vegans), IF deficiency (pernicious anemia, gastrectomy), ileal disease (Crohn's, resection), pancreatic insufficiency, and certain medications (metformin, PPIs).
